# judo-blueprint

The JUDO catalog is indexed by the kb (`kb_search`, `kb_get`). It has three parts under `catalog/`:

| Part | Holds | Trust |
|---|---|---|
| `agent-docs/` | Platform behaviour and rules, by area (model, backend, frontend, deployment, testing) | Source of truth for platform facts |
| `model-blueprints/<id>/` | Reusable domain shapes: `BLUEPRINT.md` plus `model.md` (shape and generated diagram), `backend.md`, `frontend.md` | Project-proven patterns |
| `best-practices/<domain>/<id>.md` | Single practices for model, backend and frontend | Project-proven practices |

3. **Check the platform rule** the entry links to in `agent-docs/`. When an entry and the agent-docs disagree, the agent-docs win.

## Blueprints

1. Read `BLUEPRINT.md`: *Intent*, *Constraints*, *Variations*, *When to avoid*. Pick the variation that fits.
2. Read `model.md`. `## Model shape` lists the elements; the generated Mermaid diagram shows the structure. The diagram is derived from a validated fragment model (`fragments/<id>.model` in the catalog repo), so the shape is known to pass strict validation.
3. Rename to your domain. Entry names are neutral examples (`Partner`, `Order`); keep the relation kinds, cardinalities, flags and expressions.
4. Build it with the JUDO model CLI skill in your project (one writer per model file), then `validate --strict`.
5. Read `backend.md` and `frontend.md` only for the layers you touch. Code there is **example project code** unless it says *platform*: adapt it, do not copy class names that do not exist in your project.

## Best practices

Read *Practice* and *Example*. The *Rationale* says why; *When to avoid* says when not to. Follow the links into `agent-docs/` for the platform rule behind it.

## Platform vs project practice

Entries separate the two:

- **Platform**: behaviour of the generated code and runtime, backed by `agent-docs/`. Rely on it.
- **Project practice**: what one or more projects chose. Adopt it if it fits; it is not enforced by the platform.
- **Verify** notes mark claims the agent-docs do not settle (for example the transaction scope of a custom operation). Test them in your project before relying on them.

# Build Process

This guide covers the build system, Maven configuration, and build workflows for the <app> project.

## Table of Contents

- [judo.sh Script](#judosh-script)
- [Maven Commands](#maven-commands)
- [Build Profiles](#build-profiles)
- [Build Workflows](#build-workflows)
- [Maven Configuration](#maven-configuration)
- [Troubleshooting](#troubleshooting)
- [Best Practices](#best-practices)

## Key Concepts

- **Model Transformation**: The process of converting the high-level ESM model into more detailed PSM and ASM models, which are then used to generate code.
- **SDK Generation**: The process of creating the Java interfaces and DTOs that make up the application's API.
- **Reckless Mode**: A fast build mode for development that skips non-essential checks and enables caching.
- **Hot Deployment**: A development feature that allows you to apply backend code changes without restarting the server.

## Project Template and Generation

### How the Project is Generated

This project was initially generated using the **JUDO ESM Fullstack Project Template**:
- **Template Repository**: [judo-esm-fullstack-project-template](https://github.com/BlackBeltTechnology/judo-esm-fullstack-project-template)
- **Generator**: Uses Handlebars templates to create project skeleton
- **Configuration**: `generator-p

### `judo.sh build` flag reference

`./judo.sh build` accepts a set of single-letter flags whose semantics are non-obvious — several look like "only X" but actually mean "skip X". Inverting them is a common source of "my model change isn't taking effect" reports.

| Flag | Long form | Meaning |
|------|-----------|---------|
| `-M` | `--skip-model` | **Skip** the ESM→PSM→ASM→RDBMS→keycloak transformation. NOT "model only". Use when iterating on Java backend only. |
| `-B` | `--skip-backend` | Skip backend (custom ops + interceptors + REST). |
| `-F` | `--skip-frontend` | Skip frontend React generation/build. |
| `-KA` | `--skip-karaf` | Skip Karaf assembly. |
| `-S` | `--skip-schema` | Skip schema-migration image. |
| `-a` | `--build-app-module` | Build the `app/` (custom ops) module only — fast iteration on Java code. |
| `-f` | `--build-frontend-module` | Build the frontend React module only. |
| `-sc` | `--build-schema-cli` | Build the standalone schema CLI JAR. |
| `-d` | `--docker` | Build Docker images. |
| `-p` | `--build-parallel` | Maven parallel build (noisier logs). |
| `-q` | `--quick` | Quick mode — use cache, skip validations. |
| `-i` | `--ignore-checksum` | Ignore generator checksum errors and refresh checksums. |

**Model-only changes need a full `./judo.sh build` (no flags) — not `-M`.** The `asm2keycloak` transformation (which emits the Keycloak realm from your `ActorType`s) onl

# Local Development

This guide covers local development setup, hot deployment, and development workflows for the <app> project.

## Table of Contents

- [Development Server Setup](#development-server-setup)
- [Hot Deployment](#hot-deployment)
- [Development Workflow](#development-workflow)
- [Monitoring and Debugging](#monitoring-and-debugging)
- [Troubleshooting](#troubleshooting)
- [Best Practices](#best-practices)

## Key Concepts

- **Hot Deployment**: A development feature that allows you to apply backend code changes to a running server without a full restart. This enables a fast and efficient development loop.
- **Karaf Console**: A command-line interface for the running Karaf server. It allows you to manage bundles, view logs, and change configurations at runtime.
- **Incremental Builds**: The process of building only the modules that have changed, rather than the entire application. This significantly speeds up the development workflow.

## Development Server Setup

### Starting Services

**Start all services:**
```bash
./judo.sh start
```

**What starts:**
- PostgreSQL (Docker container, port 5432)
- Keycloak (Docker container, port 8080)
- Karaf (standalone, HTTP port **8181** by default; override with `./judo.sh start -o karaf_port=<port>`)

**Access points:**
- Application: http://localhost:8181/apps/
- Keycloak Admin: h

# JUDO Model CLI

The `judo-cli.jar` is the JUDO platform's command-line interface to the model. Every read of and change to the ESM **must** go through this tool — never by editing the model files directly.

## Identity

| | |
|---|---|
| **Artifact** | `judo-cli.jar` |
| **Location** | `target/judo-cli.jar` (downloaded by `./judo.sh build`; Maven coordinate `hu.blackbelt.judo.runtime:judo-model-cli:${judo-model-cli-version}`) |
| **Invocation** | `java -jar target/judo-cli.jar -m model/<app>.model <command>` (a `judo_cli` shell alias is not available in non-interactive shells) |
| **Source ESM** | `model/<app>.model` |
| **Interface** | GraphQL over the ESM (and over the derived models, when loaded) |

## Why a CLI is mandatory

The ESM is stored as a single XMI file (`model/<app>.model`). Three properties of that file make it unsafe to read or edit by hand:

- **Size.** A typical project's ESM contains tens of thousands of XMI elements. Reading the file is expensive in token budget and dangerous in attention — patterns spread across thousands of lines are easily missed.
- **Internal cross-references.** Elements reference each other by `xmi:id`. A single hand edit in one part of the file can silently dangle a reference somewhere else, with no immediate error.
- **Schema validation.** The metam

# Code Generation Maven Plugins and Workflow Goals

This guide describes the Maven plugins that drive code generation in the JUDO build. Most developers use them indirectly through `./judo.sh generate` and `./judo.sh build`; read this when you need to call a goal directly or debug generation.

## Raw Generation Flags

`./judo.sh generate` runs Maven `compile` with the VM flags `-DgenerateRoot=true -DgenerateApplication=true -DskipApplicationBuild=true`; updating versions adds `-DupdateJudoVersions=true`. New-project scaffolding runs the `create-judo-project` goal of `judo-version-updater-maven-plugin` with `-DmodelName`, `-DprojectGroupId`, `-DprojectArtifactId`, `-DprojectVersion` and `-Dversion`. The backend is started with `clean install` in `application/karaf-offline` plus `-DrunKaraf=true`.

## ESM Generator Plugin (`judo-esm-generator-maven-plugin`)

| Goal | Default phase | Purpose |
|------|---------------|---------|
| `generate` | `generate-resources` | Generate the project from the ESM model |
| `create` | none (`requiresProject=false`) | Standalone creation of a project |
| `checksum` | `generate-resources` | Compute checksums |
| `clean` | `clean` | Delete tracked generated files |
| `resetChecksum` | `clean` | Rewrite stored checksums |
| `synchronizeGitignore` | `proces

`model/webshop.model`. The backend, REST API, database schema

customization points the generator leaves for it.

title: accesspoint Package Reference
description: Reference of the ESM accesspoint package: actors, claims, principals, accesses and security behaviour rules.
---

# `accesspoint` Package Reference

**[◄ Back to Index](./README.md)**

This package defines the security model for the application, including user roles (actors), permissions, and access control mechanisms. It specifies *who* can do *what*.

---

## Element Reference

### `ActorType`
Defines a role or type of user/system that can interact with the application (e.g., `Administrator`, `SalesAgent`, `Customer`). It aggregates all security-related definitions for that role.

| Attribute / Reference | Type | Cardinality | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `name` | String | `[1]` | The name of the actor role. |
| `principal` | `TransferObjectType` | `[0..1]` | A reference to the TO that holds the identifying information for this actor (e.g., a `UserAccount` TO). |
| `claims` | `Claim` | `[0..*]` | A list that defines which attributes of the `principal` are used for identity purposes (e.g., mapping the `email` attribute to the `EMAIL` claim type). |
| `accesses` | `Access` | `[0..*]` | The list of data access permissions granted to this actor. This defines what data the actor can see. |
| `menuItems` | `MenuItem` | `[0..*]` | The root of the navigation menu structure that will be visible to this actor. |
| `kind` | `ActorKind` Enum | `[1]` | The type of actor: `HUMAN` (an interactive user) or `SYSTEM` (an

# JUDO Model Development Guide

This document is the primary reference for understanding and working with the JUDO application model. It synthesizes foundational concepts, a practical workflow for getting started, and a comprehensive catalog of advanced design patterns for solving real-world modeling problems.

## Table of Contents
1.  [**Part 1: Foundational Concepts**](#part-1-foundational-concepts)
2.  [**Part 2: The Core Modeling Workflow**](#part-2-the-core-modeling-workflow)
3.  [**Part 3: Advanced Modeling Patterns & Use Cases**](#part-3-advanced-modeling-patterns--use-cases)
4.  [**Part 4: Judo Query Language (JQL)**](#part-4-judo-query-language-jql)
5.  [**Part 5: Rules and Best Practices**](#part-5-rules-and-best-practices)

---

## Part 1: Foundational Concepts

### 1.1 The JUDO Two-Layer Architecture

JUDO models have a mandatory two-layer logical structure that promotes abstraction, security, and reusability.

*   **Domain Logic Layer**: This is the core of the application. It contains the **`EntityType`s`**, which are the classes that represent the real-world business objects and rules. This layer is the single source of truth for how data is structured, persisted, and changed.

*   **Service Layer**: This layer acts as a front-facing interface or API for the applic

# UI Authoring Guide

**[◄ Back to Index](./README.md)**

This guide is the **authoring playbook** for composing ESM UI presentations (Form, Table, View) on `TransferObjectType`s, and for wiring them onto an `ActorType` menu. It is the companion to the per-element reference in [ui.md](./esm_metamodel/ui.md).

**Scope boundary.** This file explains **what to put in the model**. For how to actually mutate the model, see [judo-cli.md](./judo-cli.md). For per-element attribute definitions, enums, and conditional rules, see [ui.md](./esm_metamodel/ui.md) and [ui-behaviour.md](./esm_metamodel/ui-behaviour.md). For customizing the **generated React** once the UI model is in place, see the [frontend documentation](../frontend/README.md).

---

## TL;DR — Invariants every authoring step must respect

1.  **Every mapped `TransferObjectType` that participates in UI scaffolds exactly three siblings:** `<form>`, `<table>`, `<view>`. These three tags are typed by **containment** on `TransferObjectType` and therefore carry **no `xsi:type`**.
2.  **`xsi:type` is required iff the containing EReference is abstract.** See the Concrete-vs-Abstract Containment table below.
3.  **Every UI leaf that shows data must bind to a `structure::` e

[130] TOOLRESULT: ## 2. Security & UI Patterns

### Pattern: Mapped Managed Principal with Claim Mapping
*   **Use Case**: You want the logged-in user to correspond to a **persisted row** in your domain model (e.g. a `User` entity carrying roles, permission flags, preferences, audit fields), and you want JUDO to keep that row in sync with the IdP automatically — no manual "just-in-time user provisioning" interceptor for the common case.
*   **Problem**: Without claim mapping the only way to reach identity data is the raw JWT via `USER.*`. That forces every access filter and custom op to re-derive the user row from a JWT claim (`User!filter(u | u.email == String!getVariable('USER', 'email'))!any()`), and gives you no persisted identity at all — making roles, audit, and per-user flags impossible to model.
*   **Pattern**: Combine three ESM features on the `accesspoint` side so the framework takes over provisioning and exposes the persisted row to JQL as `ACTOR.*`:
    1.  **`ActorType.principal`** points at a **mapped** `TransferObjectType` that projects the `EntityType` that stores the user row (e.g. a `User` TO mapped to `User` entity).
    2.  **`ActorType.managed = true`**. On `#_principal` the framework is *intended* to insert (first login) or update (subsequent logins) the principal row from the JWT. **In practice the auto-insert is NOT implemented in the current runtime** — see the warning below — so you must pair `managed=true` with an `AuthenticationInterceptor` that performs the first-
[131] CALL bash: {"command": "cd /Users/bmiller/Projects/judo-blueprint/catalog/agent-docs && grep -n -B3 -A8 \"test-\\[\\?users\\|test-users-realm\" deployment/generator-plugins.md | cut -c1-700 | head -40", "timeout": 20}

[164] TOOLRESULT: start_keycloak () {
    local INSTANCE_NAME=$1
    local KEYCLOAK_PORT=$2

    docker_instance_exists $INSTANCE_NAME
    if [ $? -eq 0 ]; then
      echo "Instance $INSTANCE_NAME is not existing, starting..."
      keycloak_image=quay.io/keycloak/keycloak:23.0
      tcp_port_is_open 127.0.0.1 $KEYCLOAK_PORT
      if [ $? -eq 0 ]
      then
          echo "Could not start Keycloak. Port is already used: ${KEYCLOAK_PORT}"
          exit 1
      fi

      local keycloakExtraEnvs=""
      if [ $postgres -eq 1 ]; then
            keycloakExtraEnvs=$(echo "-e KC_DB=postgres -e KC_DB_URL_HOST=postgres-${schema_name} -e KC_DB_URL_DATABASE=${schema_name} -e KC_DB_PASSWORD=${schema_name} -e KC_DB_USERNAME=${schema_name} -e KC_DB_SCHEMA=public")
      fi

      create_docker_network ${app_name}
      docker run -d \
          --name ${INSTANCE_NAME} \
          --network=${app_name} \
          -e KEYCLOAK_ADMIN=admin \
          -e KEYCLOAK_ADMIN_PASSWORD=judo ${keycloakExtraEnvs} \
          -p $KEYCLOAK_PORT:$KEYCLOAK_PORT \
          -it $keycloak_image \
          start-dev --http-port=$KEYCLOAK_PORT --http-relative-path /auth || exit
    else
        start_docker_instance $INSTANCE_NAME
    fi
}

# 1 - instance name

[392] CALL kb_get: {"path": "agent-docs/frontend/esm-to-ui-mappings/tables-navigation.md", "section": "ESM to UI Mapping: Tables & Navigation > Table-Specific Elements > DataColumn (ui:DataColumn)"}
[393] TOOLRESULT: 
**Supertypes:** `Column` → `VisualElement` → `NamedElement`

A column within a table displaying a single attribute for each row.

| ESM Element | ESM Attribute | UI Model | Frontend Effect |
|-------------|---------------|----------|-----------------|
| `ui:DataColumn` | `name` *(generated, read-only)* | Column name | Column identifier — runtime value derived from `dataFeature.member.name` by a `getName()` EOperation override; XMI `name="…"` is stored but ignored on read and FQN computation |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `label` | Header text | Column header (i18n) |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `iconName` | Column icon | Header icon |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `dataFeature` | Bound attribute | Data binding |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `width` | Column width | Fixed pixel width |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `flex` | Flex value | Responsive width |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `sortable` | Allow sorting | Sort icon visibility |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `defaultSort` | Initial sort | `ASC`/`DESC`/`NONE` |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `filterable` | Allow filtering | Filter in column menu |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `hidden` | Hidden state | Column not displayed |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `align` | Text alignment | `LEFT`/`CENTER`/`RIGHT` |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `isPinnable` | Allow pinning | Pin column to left/right |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `formatValue` | Apply formatting | Format numeric values |
| `ui:DataColumn` | `customImplementation` | Custom column | Extension point for custom rendering |

**Default Sort Values:**

[406] TOOLRESULT: ## Custom Columns

### Purpose

Add custom columns to tables with custom rendering logic. Useful for computed values, status indicators, or action buttons.

### Basic Implementation

```typescript
// src/custom/hooks/columnCustomizer.tsx
import type { ColumnCustomizerHook } from '~/utilities';
import { GridRenderCellParams } from '@mui/x-data-grid';
import { Chip } from '@mui/material';
import type { ServiceLocationStored } from '~/services/data-api/model/ServiceLocation';

export const locationStatusColumn: ColumnCustomizerHook<ServiceLocationStored> = (original) => ({
  ...original,
  field: 'qualityScore',
  headerName: 'Validation Status',
  width: 180,
  renderCell: (params: GridRenderCellParams) => {
    const quality = params.row.qualityScore;
    const colorMap = {
      'HIGH': 'success',
      'MEDIUM': 'warning',
      'LOW': 'info',
      'NONE': 'error'
    };

    return (
      <Chip
        label={quality}
        color={colorMap[quality] || 'default'}
        size="small"
      />
    );
  },
});
```

### Registration

```typescript
// src/custom/application-customizer.tsx
import {
  TABLE_COLUMN_CUSTOMIZER_HOOK_INTERFACE_KEY,
  type ColumnCustomizerHook,
} from '~/utilities';
import { locationStatusColumn } from './hooks/columnCustomizer';

context.registerService<ColumnCustomizerHook<ServiceLocationStored>>(
  TABLE_COLUMN_CUSTOMIZER_HOOK_INTERFACE_KEY,
  locationStatusColumn,
  { component: 'ServiceLocationsTable', column: 'qualityScore' }
);
```

[721] ASSISTANT: Level 0 (S01–S06) is built and running. S03 create and update were checked with curl, not in the browser. I also could not confirm Back on the details page.

**Run it**
- **App URL:** http://localhost:8181/webshop/Shop/. The `/apps/` page in the docs only lists bundles.
- **Login:** `shop` / `shop`. Keycloak admin is `admin` / `judo` at http://localhost:8280/auth.
- **Start:** `cd ~/Projects/webshop-test && ./judo.sh start`. It stays in the foreground, so use nohup or a separate terminal. The app takes about 2 minutes to come up.
- **Stop:** `./judo.sh stop`. Then kill the leftover `judo.sh start` and `tail` processes.
- **Ports and database:** I set `keycloak_port=8280` because 8080 was taken by another container, and `dbtype=postgresql` in `judo.properties`. Postgres is on 5432.

**What I checked**
- **S01:** The app URL opens Keycloak login, and after login the shop home page loads (browser).
- **S02:** The create form shows Price, Name and Description (browser).
- **S03:** I created and updated products by curl. "Running shoe" at 89.90 became 79.90, and I added "Trail backpack" and "Wool socks". In the browser, my attempt to create through the form failed with a 400. I traced that to the browser tool setting the numeric field, not to the app.
- **S04:** The list shows 79.90, 12.00 and 129.50 (browser).
- **S05:** The details page shows the multi-line description, name and price (browser). Price there reads `129.5`, not two decimals. The Back button is visible, but my clicks did not register, so I could not test it.
- **S06:** After `./judo.sh stop` and `start`, all three products were still there (curl and browser).

**Main gaps** (full list in `GAPS.md`, catalog paths per function in `TRACE.md`)
1. **First login user:** the catalog has no recipe for it. I created `shop` in Keycloak through its admin API and inserted the matching `t_webshop_shop_user` row with psql. A fresh Keycloak or database loses the user.
2. **Project creation:** the catalog gives no plugin groupId or version for `create-judo-project`. I took the version from `~/.m2`.
3. **CLI jar:** `target/judo-cli.jar` is not created by `generate-root`. I got it with `mvn -DgenerateRoot validate`.
4. **Two-decimal price:** there is no model-level way to get it. I patched one generated table file and listed it in `.generator-ignore`.
5. **Long text:** the predefined `Text` type is only 250 characters. I added a `LongText` type (4000).
6. **Other doc errors and omissions:** a TransferObjectType mapped to an entity creates its members and pages by itself, so my hand-added members were duplicates. The default `dbtype` is `hsqldb`, not Postgres. Keycloak runs under `/auth`. The `/apps/` URL is not the app. A frontend-only rebuild does not refresh the unpacked Karaf assembly.

I did not read `structure.md` or `model-screen-layout.md`. Everything is committed locally and nothing was pushed. I stopped at Level 0.

[730] TOOLRESULT: 
A user who logs in through Keycloak must have an application user row (the actor's entity) before the runtime can resolve the actor. Instead of registering users by hand, an `AuthenticationInterceptor` creates the row on the first login from the token claims. This is just-in-time (JIT) provisioning.

[737] TOOLRESULT: 
### Platform: the hook and what the runtime does itself

- `AuthenticationInterceptor` (not `OperationCallInterceptor`) has `getName`, `isSuitableForOperation(operation, claim, realm, client, attributes)` and `authenticate(operationFQName, exchange, claim, realm, client, attributes)`. The `attributes` map holds the claims of the JWT (`email`, `preferred_username`, `given_name`, `family_name`, `sub`) ([interface](../../agent-docs/backend/authentication-guide.md#authenticationinterceptor-interface), [token attributes](../../agent-docs/backend/authentication-guide.md#token-attribute-extraction)).
- It is called on the framework-level `#_principal` operation after a successful external login, after the principal is extracted and **before** the framework loads the mapped actor, so the row it inserts is the one the lookup then reads ([the #_principal operation](../../agent-docs/backend/authentication-guide.md#the-_principal-operation)).
- The actor row is read from the database on every request that carries a principal. No row gives `AccessDeniedException` with `AUTHENTICATED_ENTITY_NOT_FOUND`; the generated frontend shows "User not found" ([principal-to-actor resolution](../../agent-docs/backend/authentication-guide.md#principal-to-actor-resolution-and-multi-client-mapping)).
- The platform guide records that a mapped, managed actor with `<claims>` is meant to be inserted by the runtime, but the verified runtime versions do **not** insert it, so this interceptor is required even

Deep entity hierarchies (for example galaxy, star, planet) make the browser back button an unreliable way to go up one level. A navigation hook that records a breadcrumb stack gives consistent back behavior and a visible trail.

[824] CALL kb_get: {"path": "agent-docs/frontend/hooks/navigation-and-access.md", "section": "Navigation and Access Control Hooks > Type-Safe Navigation > Basic Usage"}
[825] TOOLRESULT: 
`useJudoNavigation()` is exported from `~/components`. It returns `{ navigate, clearNavigate, externalNavigate, navigateBackTo, back, isBackDisabled, setTitle }`. `navigate(to)` takes a react-router `To` (a path string or `{ pathname, search }`) and applies the registered navigation interceptor; the generated route helpers from `~/routes` (for example `routeToServiceEntityView(...)`) build the paths.

```typescript
import { useJudoNavigation } from '~/components';

export const QuickActions: FC = () => {
  const { navigate, back } = useJudoNavigation();

  return (
    <Box>
      <Button onClick={() => navigate('/service/campaigns/create')}>New Campaign</Button>
      <Button onClick={back}>Back</Button>
    </Box>
  );
};
```
